A. VISIT TO VALE ROYAL.

A heavy, impenetrable mist enveloped the town the other morning and depressed the spirits of the members of the Warrington Y.M.C..A. Cycling club, who had arranged a run to Vale Royal. It was with some misgivings ae to the weather that the club set c-ff a little after the eppointed hour, but as they etruggled up the steeps ot Stretton, the clouds broke, the eta eppeared aid the mists die- rereed, revealing the great improvement that had taken place in the couutry since the previu4 run of the club along this route. The hedge-rows are in full leaf, the trees are putting forth their first sheets, the field crops are putting their beads above the ground, and house and cottage gardens are adorning themselves with their first summer beauty. What a change, too, there was in the fields. Whereas a month ego the country resounded with the sounds of industry, all was now remark- ably serene and nothing disturbed the rural quiet save the sudden light of birds, the cerolieg of larks, the weird cry of the cuckoo, and the gentle ripple of the breeze in the fast developing trees. 'The recent rains, besides benefiting the ceops, had also greatly improved the roads, and throughout the run,the surface of the various highways WWI in excellent ,condition. Consequently a somewhat faqt pace was maintained, and the running was much enjoyed. The first. dismount took piece at the " caution' beard at Acton Bridge over the Weaver, but soon the Club weae, speeding on their way amid tbe charming scenery of the Weaver Valley, along which the clear atmosphere permitted evensive vistas. Rising into Weaverham en,ther dismount -esccurred at a stiff, wirding hill, but, once at the top. the party mounted into their saddle and bounded off towares their deetination. After a good ride into -Cuddington, the Club left the Tarporley rood, and turning to the lett over Culdingtcn etationsran down a quiet country road which brought them to the uninaposiag entrance of Vale Royal. Passing through the gateway,a wonderful panorama revealed itself, and in order to enjoy to the full the treat afforded, the party ele.cted to walk across the patk. The undulating country was covered with withered bracken, amidst which eutrerous rabbits disported themselves. Follov.ing the read the club found there.,elves in an avenue of trees through which they roee to the teeter gate disturb- ing b- rcdents at every step. Crossing the road and passing through a wicket gate, they proceeded along a narrow path to a. second mere of smaller dimensions then the previous one, but which pre- eented a mere peaceful and secluded aspect. In Camp, Club, and Cottage

Bovril is aliJe esteemed as the perfection of con- densed food, strengthening, refreshing, sustaining ; a stimulant before and a restorative after fatigue; an invaluable aid to the club chef and to the thrifty household manager; a helper to the athlete; a treat to the epicure ; a boon to the invalid. The purity and strength of Bovril have been vouched for by the most eminent scientists, and so nutritive and stimulative are its properties that it has been aptly described as "liquid Life."

Tourists, Travellers, Cyclists, and others who may be contemplating extended trips during the forthcoming holiday season should be the first to avail themselves of the strengthening and sustaining qualities of Bovril. When on a jour- ney, when yachting, mountaineering, riding, walking, cycling, Bovril will be found not only to act as a • welcome " Pick-me-up" after undue

fatigue ; but taken in the morning before starting, or between meals, it acts as a splendid stimulant and sustenant, staying hunger and maintaining the physical energies to the full.

===== 0002601_18990519_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
Good Cookery

is the art of embodying in the most palatable, appet- ising, and dainty form all the nutritive properties belonging to wholesome food. With Bovril the resourceful housewife can make the most delicious, nourishing soups at the shortest notice. Entrees of every description, stews, hashes, ragouts, almis, are rendered doubly acceptable when Bovril is employed as an ingredient. Once used in the kitchen Bovril holds a permanent place 2- s a culinary necessity ; because, whilst being eminently nutri- tious and of the most delicate flavour, it is both money-saving and time-saving.

THE REV. JOSEPH ELLISON IN

AMERICA

A TESTIMONIAL.

We have received from the Rev. Joseph Ellison, youngest eon •of Councillor Lek Ellison, Latchford. a ,copy ef the testimonial presented to him on Sunday, April 30th, by the memb-rs ot the Baptist Church, Carver, Mass, where Mr. Ellison has been pastor for over four years, and has now left to continue his work in San. Francisco. Be informs us that there has been great unaminity cf feeling in Carver, on his behalf, and on Apiil 20th a giand concert was given in his honour, the procetda of which were handed over to him. The testimonial present d t) Mr. Ellison reads as follows : "To the clergymen and brethiee in California : This certifies that our t rother. the Rev. Joseph Eltison, the bearer 01 this paper, has revienecl his pastorate with us, and feels it to be his duty to continua the preaching of the gospel in the West. We therefore unite in this testimonial relative to his character, as a man and minister while without people. Upwards of four years and four months he has sustained an irreproachable character in onscammunity, and taught the Word with excellent ability, to the eeification of his church and people. The church parts with an afieble man, an ex- celeet pastor, epee who has advised with us and for us, and leaves with many regrets, yet in peace and harmony. Ile is a clean and active advo- ceie in his Master's service, is a progreFsive student, a devetcl Christian, and up to date in setting forth the tru4o; and no t.ireums'Ances, however humiliatinZ, deur him from any pa.torate duty. We cordially recom- mend him to the fellowship of any church of our faith and practise, whose wateheare he may aecepti, and earnestly pray that God's grace may be his bulwark of power in ether fields, as well as our own.—Sired) FEENEZER S. LUCAr3, JAMES A. VAUGHAN, E. ALLAN Lucce, Committee ot Church. May Ist, 1899.

Malts, P.N.G., for past services rendered to the Leilge, Bro J. Apple- ton made the presentation, welch consisted of.an emble-i of the Orier, and in a brief speech said he trusted Bro. Mains would value the gilt, not for its value alone, hut for tte honour which was attached to such a presentntion. He expressed the hope that Bro. LADIES' LETTER.

ME DEAR NELLIE, People who are of normal proportions find no difficulty nowadays in getting smart and inexpen- sive frocks ready made, but there are many who prefer to have their dresses made to measure, and to their own design, and who find a difficulty in getting this done satisfactorily in a small torn ab a moderate expense.

Very effective is a long coat which is bnilb primarily of rich silk moird striped with pale gold satin, the whited stripe having for its further adornment a design of violets grouped in careless bunches. The edge of the coat is cut in deep points, and then comes a flounce formed of lace and pale gold ribbon, which falls over an under- frill of accordion-pleated gold silk, itself finished with a small ruche at the hem. A large vandyked collar is also edged with a flounce of lace and ribbon corresponding to that which finishes off the wrists of the prettily shaped sleeves, which, by the way, show a considerable difference in size to those worn last season. Tiny triple ruches of chiffon outline every point of the coat, while the high collar is a billowy mass of the pale gold chiffon that forms so bewitching a back- ground for a pretty face. The whole coat is lined throughout with the le shade of glac4.

"WOMEN'S CHAT."

At the last Drawing Room, which was held by Her Majesty in perssn, transparent trains were much en evidence. Those worn by esiou,antes were all billowy ma-se' of chiffon and tulle, the effect being beautiful in the extreme. These nt w trains bang quite as gracefully as the old-fashioned silk, satin. velvet, or brocade speci- mens, and the difference in weight is an item much ap- preciatsd by the wearers. A thin silver wire is inserted all round, and this is the only support deemed neces- sary. The simplicity of the eebutantes' gown, governs its cost, which rarely exceeds seventeen or eighteen guineas. And when it has served its turn it is always converted into an evening dress or ball gown. The de- butantes' Court dress is white, of course, white trim- mings, white flowers being always employed. When the gown is ordered, the next thing to do is ti learn to curt- sey, that is, if the art has not already been acquir d at school, and when both are reads', nothing rem ins but to await with what patience at cornmand—the eventful day. Mendelssohn, it is well known, wsuld not Beast' even to gain the appreleation of Royalty, but he as charmee with the Qu eft's performance, and could not restrain himself from telling her so. Princeem Chrislan is beeinning to look somewhat weary and tired, arid little wond r, coevidr ring, the way in which she has been rushing from orse place to another to perform the divers duties she had set herself to carry out. Few women in any rai.k of life work harder than Princess Christian. Of the many associations which have been organised by her, probably the Royal British Nnrses' Association is the must far reaching. The Prin- eess is president, but that only begins her connection with this excellent society, which owes its existence to her untiringfenergy,and it wasmntirely through her inlu- Klee that it was granted a Royal Charter iii 1893. Miss Florence Nightirgale, who has just entered her eightieth year, wee trained art a nurse by the Protestant Sister of Mercs at Kdserwerth on the Rhine. THE SEAFARERS

A MODERN ROMANCE.

BY JOHN BLOUNDELLE-BURTON, Author of "Fortune's my Foe," "The Scourge of God," "Across the Salt Seas," "The Clash of Arms," "In the Day of Adversity," "A Gentleman Adventurer, " "His Own Enemy," "Denounced,"

SYNOPSIS OF PRECEDING CH&PTERS: Arabella Waldron, a young girl, ia to sail for India in a ship belonging to her uncle, Captain Pooley, in order to be married at Bombay. But, previously, the reader is to be told of her courtship. A year before, the Captain had taken her to the theatre, whore she had met Stephen Charke, a young friend of her uncle, who is smitten by her. Afterwards, at a lunch at Portsmouth, Bella meets Gill,ert Bampfyld, a flag- lieutenant to the Rear-Admiral, and at a ball in the evening she gives him most of her dances. After starting for Bombay to marry Gilbert, who is stationed on a warship there, a cable comes for Bella not to start, as the wanliip has been ordered to Africa. She is, however, already out of reach. But, on the way to India, the ship meets a slave dhow, and on that dhow, mysteriously wounded, is Gilbert Bamplyld. Bella is much pestered by the attentions of Charke, Wll9 has become first Officer of the ship, and he secretly manifests against Gilbert.

CITATTER XI. VITE TZREOR INCREASE 3.

The southerly wind did not hold as it should have done considering the time of year, and the consequence was that the Emperor of the Moon was by no means making the passage that was to be expected of her. Indeed, by the time that the second day had passed since the rescue of Gilbert from the slaver, and when the evening was at hand, she was almost motionless on the water, and with such sails as were still left standing hanging as listlessly as though they were suspended in a back room. Now, this was disheartening to all on board—that is to say, to all except one person— as is generally the case when such things happen. The master was grieved because he looked upon the delay as an absolute waste of valuable time, while as for Bella and Gilbert—well! it is scarcely neces- sary to write down here what they were looking forward to at the end of their journey, nor what visions haunted the mind of the latter of the Cathedral in Bombay and a ceremony of marriage being performed at the altar rails by the Bishop. Yet, all—passengers, master, officers and men, had to swallow their disappointment as best they might, and to recognise the fact that Bombay was still over three thousand miles away and not likely to be reached for very many days. The one person who, however, was resigned to the affliction of delay was Stephen Charke, in whose brain there still lingered a wild and chimer- ical idea that there might yet be sent by Fate some extraordinary piece of good fortune which would, even at the last moment, sever Gilbert and Bella, with the subsequent result of bringing him, Stephen and Bella, together. It has been said that he was a dreamer, and never had he been so more than now, since, sleeping and waking, he still mused on the possibility of some extraordinary set of circumstances arising which should force the girl into his arms. Yet, he had to own to himself, that nothing was more unlikely than that any such circumstances could by any possibility arise. If anything visited these seas, this stupendous ocean, at this period of the year, it was more likely to be a flat calm such as that which they were now experiencing, instead of storms; and, even if storms should come, of what avail would they be to separate Gilbert Bampfylcl and Bella 'Waldron. "I am a fool! " he would mutter to himself, as he smoked his pipe either in the solitude of his own cabin or on the deck at night, "a fool. A. madman! One has only to observe how they love each other, how they never leave each other's side, to see that nothing could ever bring her to me. Even though she and I were cast on some deserted shore, even though I saved her from forty thousand threatened deaths—even though Bampfyld himself were dead and buried, she would never give herself to me. I am," he would repeat again, "a fool." And this acknowledgment would, for a time, operate wholesomely on him—a man whose mind was not altogether that of a visionary and whose heart was not, by nature, a perverted or warped one—and he would resolve that, hence- forth, he would think no more of this girl for whom his love was so fierce and, to him, so dis- turbing. He made resolutions, therefore, and kept them—until the next time that he saw the lovers together, smiling, talking, happy in each other!—"billing and cooing," as he called it, with a smothered curse.

Ff They were on deck, now, together, on the evening of the second day when Stephen went up to take the first watch, for Gilbert had refused to remain shut up in the cabin allotted to him for more than twenty-four hours, and Pooley also was there, Fagg being below finish- ing his supper. Mrs. Pooley sat on the poop in a deck-chair engage Ain some needlework she had constantly on hand, and, forward, the men were engaged in smoking and telling yarns, while the general idleness which pervades the fo'castle when a ship is becalmed, prevailed everywhere. One man was reading a short story to his mates out of a country paper six months old, another had a sewing-machine between his legs with which he was mending his and his comrades' clothes, a third was teasing and playing with "Bengalee," the tiger cub, which was growing—or seemed to be growing—fast. At present, however, it was safe to let it loose since it had no more streng:h than a large sized cat, and teeth not much bigger than those domestic animals possess. Generally, the creature followed Bella about wherever she went, rolling down the companion ladder after her like a striped ball when she went below, or lying on the edge of her dress when she sat on deck ; but at night it was shut up in a locker forward and looked after by the sailors. The hour for its tem- poia7 retirement had not, however, yet arrived, wherefore it was still gambolling about amongst the men.

Altogether the vessel presented a peaceful scene as she lay "idle as a painted ship upon a painted ocean," while from forward there came the droning voice of the sailor who was reading the storyette to his mates, interrupted only by the laughter of the others at the cub's leaps and growls, and from the after part of the ship there arose the talk of the quarter-deck people. "Come," said Pooley, now addressing el-lark% "come, let us go and look at those unfortunate niggers. The Lord knows what is to become of them. The woman, you say, never rises from the floor of the cabin, but only lies there and moans! It is the strangest thing I ever heard of in my life. I wish, Mr. .flampfyld," turning to that gentleman as he passed with Bella, "that you could give us some information about that dhow we found you

But, of course, Gilbert could tell them no mare than ho had already done a dozen tunes, repeating in substance all that he had said to his fiancee. "I am sorry," he had said on each occasion, and again said now, "but I know absolutely nothing. I was insensible when I was taken into the dhow—as • taken in I must have been, since I could never have got in by myself—and, as you are well aware, I was insensible when I was brought out. I positively know nothing." "The helmsman's death was as strange as any- thing," Pooley. observed. "Fagg says there was no wound about him that he could see. What, therefore, could he have died of?" "Sunstroke, I imagine," the Brat mate said, in his usual emphatic, crisp manner. "Sunstroke. It could not have been fever,, otherwise these negroes would have it. too. Yet," he went on, :n a manner more meditative than usual with him, "those Arabs, if he was an Arab, rarely suffer from that. It takes a white man to get sun- stroke."

"WA. come," said the master again, "let's go and see to them. The sun is on tile horizon ;it will be dark in a quarter of an hour's tine." Whereon he strocle forward, accompanied by Charke, while Mr. Fagg, who had come •up from the saloon, began to keep such ..watch as was necessary. And Gilbert, bidding Bella go and sit with Mrs. Pooley, strode afier them anxious to have a look at the .unhappy creatures who had been rescued at 'the same time as himself.

The male blaplis had been put into a deck cabin (in which usually was kept an assortment of things such as spare lamps, a boat sail or two, and Mr. Fang's bicycle, . on which he disported himself whenever he got ashore anywhere), in which sotuo matting had been thrown down for their accom- modation. And, as now they neared this cabin, they heard sounds procerrlig from within it which wens really nmr.ne., but to their ears had more the semblance of the bleating of sheep. Also, it seethed as-if one of themen within was chanting some sort of song or incantation, . "We shall have to stop this noise in some way,

sir,'' Charke saw, to the captain ; it tins neen going on more or less ever since they came on board, and the men complain that it disturbs them 4st the fo'castie. s a pity we can't communicate with them somehow. Perhaps Lieutenant Bampfyld might try, as he says be knows some words of Swahili ' ; and as he spoke he looked at the man who bad once more, as he considered, or chose to consider, stepped in between him and the woman he loved. Yet, because he never forgot that he was a gentleman born, there was nothing in his manner that was otherwise than polite when he addressed Gilbert.

"I have tried," said the latter, "more than once to-day. And either my Swahili is defective, or that is not their language. I suspect it's Galla, myself, of which I do not know a word." Meanwhile, the captain had drawn back the cocoa- nut matting which hung in front of the deck cabin door, though, after peering into the sombre dusk for a moment, he started back, exclaiming; "Good heavens! what has happened now? Have they murdered one of their companions, or what ? " It was in truth a weird sort of scene on which he, as well as Gilbert and Charke, gazed, as the swiftly failing tropic evening light illumined the interior of the cabin. Flat on the floor of it lay one of the negroes, undoubtedly dead, since on his face was now the grey, slate-coloured hue which the African assumes in death. Yet his eyes were open, only now, instead of that bright, glassy lodk which all their eyes had had since they were brought on board, there was a dull, filmy look, which told plainly enough that there was no life behind them. Still, dead as the man undoubtedly was, he did not present the most uncanny spectacle of all within! That was furnished by those who of late had been his comrades, and by the strange grotesqueness—a grotesqueness that wag horrible in itself—of their actions. All three of the still living negroes were on their knees in the cabin, which was roomy enough to amply admit of their being so, while with their gre t black hands they were pawing the man all over, feeling his breast and body. and en- deavouring to bend his fingers and toes, and also his legs and arms; while, even as they did so, from their great mouths came that moaning incantation which resembled so much the bleating of sheep. Doubtless they felt almost sure that their fellow- slave was dead, and these actions were being per- formed ae- tests. Yet, also, there was a solemn, wild, unearthliness attached to the whole thing by the manner in which, when one of the visitors standing at the door peering in made a remark, all turned their sightless eyes towards that door, then helrl pp each a hand, and emitted a hissing noise tlp ou-sh their great. pendulous lips, as though enjoining silence and respect to the dead. Held up those hands, the fingers stretched enormously apart, the palms towards the intruders. Their eyes were bright enough as they glared at the three white men outside, whom they could not see, yet somehow the gleam in them and the knowledge that they were sightless, gave so creepy a feeling to those regarding them. that they could not restrain a shudder. "What in God's name is it ? " exclaimed Pooley, he being the first to speak. "What? What horrible disease that blinds them to commence with, and then kills—and kills not. only negro, but Arab —captured and captor? Who—which—will be the next ? "

As he spoke, a thought struck each of the three as they stood there gazing at one another in the swiftly-arrived darkness of the tropic night—a thought to which, however, not one of them gave Utterance. Who would be the neat? An Arab had died from some strange' unknown disease in the ship wherein these men had been found, and now one of them, too—a negro! Had, they reflected, this insidious horror been, therefore, brought into a ship full of white men? Would they also fall victims to that which had killed the others? That was the thought in their minds—in the minds of all of them, though not one gave voice to that thought.

"He must be got away from them—taken out of that cabin," Pooley said, his speech a little changed now ; more husky and less clear than usual. "Perhaps the woman, the negress, may be of some avail. I doubt if they will let us remove him without difficulty—though--poor blind things as they are— they could scarcely make any resistance. Go across to the other cabin, Mr. Charke, and fetch her over."

He had been anticipated in his order, however, by the chief mate ere he spoke, Charke having, through some idea of his own, already crossed the deck to the similar cabin in which the woman was placed. Therefore, as Pooley looked round to see why the mate did not answer him, he saw in the darkness that he was returning. Also he saw —even in this darkness, which was not quite all darkness yet, and also by the light of the foredeck lantern—that he looked pale and agitated. "The woman," he said, "is dead too, I believe. She is lying on the cabin floor motionless—and cold."

CHAPTER XL'.

"STRICKEN."

It was from this time that there began to creep over the ship a feeling shared by all, both fore and aft that the voyage would not end without some- thing untoward happening. What form, however, any. misfortune which might come to them would be likely to take, none were bold enough to attempt to prophesy. Yet, all the same, the feeling was there, and, since every mart on board the ship was a sailor, while, of the ladies, one was a sailor's wife and the other a sailor's future wife (each of whom was certain to be strongly receptive of the ideas and superstitions of her own particular mate), it was not very strange that such should be the ease. Also, there was still in the thoughts of all that idea to which neither of the men congregated outside the cabin when the negro had been found dead had ventured to give expression-the idea that the unknown, insidious disease which had struck him and the negress. and also, possibly, the Arab nVocla down, m ght eventually seize on them. There was, howei e at present at least, no symptoms of a! ytl,:ng of the kind happening. All on board continued well enough, and, up to the time that the man and woman had both been buried in the sea for more than twenty-four hours, no complaints were heard from anyone of feeling at alFunwell, while the three remaining blacks seemed no worse than before.

Yet it, was a pity, perhaps, that at this time the ship should still have been forced to remain becalmed and almost motionless; that, neither from south nor west any breeze blew—from the north and east there was scarcely a possibility of wind at this season I—and that, except for the strong southern current which carried her along at a considerable, though almost imperceptible rate, she hardly stirred at all. A pity, because it. gave the sailors too many idle watches wherein to talk and chatter, to spin yarns of old-time horrors which had fallen upon vess:qs in different. parts of the world, and of strange visitations which-they had either personally offered under be had "hcerd tell on," and so 'forth.

Nor aft, in the saloon, did those who, used it fail to discuss the strange circumstance of—not so much the death which had stricken the Africans— as the blindness that had fallen upon them.' And hie, Stephen Charke, better read, perhaps, than any of the others owing to his studious nature, was able to discuss the matter im,re freely than either the captain or those who sat at his table.

"I do distinctly recollect reading somewhere," the mate said one' evening, as all sat under the great afterdeck awning, fanning themselves listlessly, while Fagg worked a kind of punkah which his ingenuity_ bild devised, "I distinctly recollect reading somewhere of all thoee in a ship, on board of which was a large cargo of West. Afri(•!ni negroes bound for America, .being stricken with blindness. I wish I could recall ;here I'read it.. In that way we might be able, also, to find out how to take stepi. to nvil the, same thing happening to tv in the old Emperor," "A cheerful prospect, truly," said the captain, "if that is to happen" ; and as he spoke he roamed his eye around the tranquil, glassy sea, on which there was not so much as a ripple. "A pleasant thing, inde«l. if one-half of us get Mind and rough titre comes on. How, then, is the ship to be worked three thousand miles. Uow are the sails to be attended to?" and now he directed hit eyes aloft tc.where all the canvas was neatly furled with the exception of the studding sails. "We'll hone it won't be as bad as that, sir,°' maid Pagg. "Only the black people, and those olt of another ship than ours, seem to surfer. Until oa, of us "—by whom be meant the Europeans on lymrd—"gets affected we haven't much to fear, take it. Also, von know, sir, we can find shelter before we re:.v.;li illornbay. There tire the Seychelles, for instance, from which we are not so very far off."

"Fu-11 delay as doing that would -entail rneanx a 4.3rious loss for me," Pooley replied. "As it is, I expect, one wny or anollior, I 611E111 Wits um, vovagc out of to years." l'l hoa not," said Gilbert Dainnfyld, s2iiously, " otherwise i shall I•(,gin to think it was a pity you over came in contnct with the dhow in which you found ue."

Y,t, as tic spoY,. he &t,17 s beautiful eye,' fixed on his face, and knew that no more crowning' - mercy had ever lyspn veliehsafed to any two mortal* than had been accorded to his, sweetheart and' ;It el, ty macaw. . "Well," said Pooley, "we won't talk about thaX.- lam devoutly thankful that we were enabled, by. God's • mercy, and also Ly the aid of something which is almost a miracle, to rescue you. For the' rest a sailor must take all that comes in his way and never repine. The Emperor has been a good old tank to me; pray heaven she continues so to. the end." Then he suddenly stopped and peered forward under the awning towards the foNnistle where, beneath another awning, the sailors had been lying about, some sleeping, some chatting idly, and most of them—even to those who had dropped off—with a pipe between their lips. "What's that commotion forward?" he asked, addressing himself to Charke who, ever on the qui-- vive as became a chief officer, had sprung to hig- feet and was gazing keenly towards the foredeck. "What's the matter with the men. and why are- those three holding Was up like that?" "Forward there!" sung out Charke in a voice- like a trumpet as he, too, saw that which the. master had described, namely, three of the hands. standing up round the man named Wilks, and one- grasping him on either side, while he himself pushed his arms out before him in a manner that implied a sort of doubtinc,a helplessness on his part. "Forward there! What's the matter with that man ? " _ _ _

"He says he can't see, sir," roared back another man on the forecastle deck, pulling his hair to. Charke as he spoke. "He was asleep just now, and then, when he woke up, he asked what time o' night it was because it was so dark." "My God!" exclaimed Pooley, while the faces. of all around him took on a blanched, terrified look, and Bella, with the beautiful carnation of her lips almost white now, grasped her lover's arm. "My God!" Then he turned to Fagg and mut- tered, repeating the other's words: " Not much, to fear until one of us gets affected.' Heavens! we haven't had long to wait!" While, following Charke who had already gone. forward, and followed by Fagg, he went towards. the forecastle. His mates were bringing Wilks down the ladder now, since Charke sung out that he should be•. taken into the comparative darkness of their quarters at) once, thereby to escape the glare of the,. sun • and not one of those who were eagerly watching his descent but observed how like his. actions were to the actions of the blind negroes. when they were brought off from the dhow, and the- actions of the others to the behaviour of the mea who had assisted them to come on board. For companions directed his hands to the ladder's ropes. even as the blacks' hands had been directed; while- in each of his motions was the same hesitation.' followed by the same careful grasping of the rail, as there had been in the motions of the slaves.

"Oh Gilbert," Bella exclaimed piteously, as she clung to him, "what is going to happen? What is hanging over us? Supposing—supposing—" "What, darling ? " "That—oh! I don't dare to say what I dread. But if this terrible thing should spread all through: the ship. If uncle, if you, if all the sailors were,. attacked. And you dearest; you, my, darling." 1 "Let us hope it will not come to that. Beside*. I, personally, matter the least of any—" "Bettie!" she almost shrieked, alarmed, "when you know that those others—some of them at least.. —that those poor black creatures, have died after it. And you ,say that you matter the least of any; you, whom I love so." "I meant as regards the ship. lam not one of her officers, nor concerned in the working of her ; and, Bella, dearest Bella, don't get these dread- ful ideas into your pretty head. Never give way to. panic in an emergency. Doubtless some more will find their epsight leave them—temporarily, but it can scarcely be that all will be attacked. And as regards death following, why, those other niggers are all right, and they are just as blind as those who have died!"

It happened—as so often such things do happen in this world! that he spoke a little too soon. He hit upon the denial of the likelihood of a possi- bility occurring which, by a strange decree of fate or chance, was, at the very moment of that denial,i to OCCUr ; for, just as the repudiation of snob probability was uttered by him, and before the men helping Wilks had had time to get him com- fortably into his berth in the forecastle, there arose once more t' at strange, bleating, moaning kind of incantation from the deck-cabin in which the remaining negroes were, which had been heard before by all. And, added to it, was something more than any had, heretofore, heard, namely, a series of wild turbulent shouts in the unknown. barbaric tongue used by the Africans—shouts that seemed to issue alone from one of their throta A noise, a bellowing, in which, though on b the Emperor of the Moon there was not one persom who could understand the words that voice uttered, all recognised the tones that denote fear, terror, and misery extreme. Instantly, so stridently horrible were those cries, every one of the Englishmen about rushed towards the cabin, Pooley and Charke being the first there, while Gilbert, running forward from the after-part and along the waist, was soon by their sides. And then, looking in, they saw that the poor. blind, excited savage who was emitting those shouts had, in truth, sufficient reason for his frenzy. He seemed—was, indeed—demented as, with both his great hands, he felt all over the bodies of his comrades who were lying lifeless on the cabin deck, and presented an awful appearance to those who gazed on him as his great features worked in excitement, his vast mouth with its adornment of huge white, teeth opened and shut like a wild beast's at bay, and his blind, but brilliant, eyes glared hideously. That he was nearly mad with fright and terror was easily apparent., since, recognising without seeing that there were others near, he snarled and bit at the space in front of him, and struck out with his fists or clawed at the air with his enormous hands. "He will spring out at us directly,: Charka said, drawing to one side of the cabin. 'The fellow is mad with fear or grief." Then, ready in expedient as over, he ran the cabin door out of its slide and shut in the negro with his dead companions. Nor did he do so too soon, since, a moment later, those without heard the huge form of the man leaping towards the door; once they heard him slip' as though he had trodden on the body or one of the. limbs of those lying dead on the floor; and then came a beating and hammering on that door which seemed to promise that, in a few seconds, the panels would be dashed out and the maddened black be amongst them. "This is too awful," muttered Pooley. "Is he really gone mad, do you think ? " he asked, appeal- ing to Charke, Gilbert and Fagg at one and the same time.

"No doubt about it," they answered together. "No doubt. And if he once gets out, to the deck, sir," said Charke, "a dozen of us will not bb able to hold, him."

"We must. capture him if he does. Throw a rope round him somehow. If he were not blind, we should have to shoot him. Hal see, he has smashed open the panel! Stand by there, some of you men, to catch him as he leaps out." And, even as he spoke, the gigantic madman, with another howl, broke down the door and sprang amongst them.

BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON.—TO BUILDERS AND CONTRACTORS. The Watch Committee of the Council of the Borough of Vifarrington invite TENDERS for the erection and com- pletion of New Police Buildings and Constabulary Offices in Wilson Patten and other streets. Warringron. according to plans and specifications prepared by R. Burns Dick, Esq , Architect. 55, Northumberland-street, Newcastle-on-Tyne. The plans and specifications can be seen at the office of the Borough Engineer and Surveyor, Town Hall, Warrington, on the 23rd and until tha 30th May, between the hours of 10 a.m. and 4 p m. • During the period copies of the quantities can be obtained, upon the payment of Two Guineas for each copy, which sum will toe returned in full on the receipt ot a bona fide tender. The Architect will be in attendance at the Town Hall, on THURSDAY, the Ist day of JUNE. from 10 to 4, to offer any explanation that may be required. Tenders (which will be received only on the printed forms), endorsed " Tender for New Police Buildings, Ac.", are to be delivered at my office, Town Hall, on or before Thursday, the 15th June.

J. LYON WHIT rLE, Town Clerk.

THE EX kA4 IN ER,

MAY Ith 1899.

THE condition of affairs in the Affairs in the Transvaal is calculated to cause Transvaal. uneasiness in this country. We need not believe too implicitly the latest account of a conspiracy in Johannesburg and the discovery of a roll of two thousand persons willing to fight against the South African Government in the in- terests of the Outlanders. Doubts upon such a Point are sure to remain until they are dispelled by much better information than any which has yet come to hand. The idea that there were British officers from Natal who were exploring or ' filibustering at sollanuesburg may be dismissed without a moment's hesitation. The men were residents, and they seem to have been betrayed by a spy, who may very well have got up the whole affair, in accordance with tactics with which the secret police are familiar in most countries. To create a conspiracy in order to denounce it is a favourite plan, and as this so-called consr- rae y has been known for tour months we may be warranted in suspecting that the whole affair is what is vulgarly known as a "put-up job." Presiden t KRUGER himself may not have been privy to it. Nevcrtbele.s. it is suspiciuns that at a time when the Giatlar..aers were remonstrating by petition, and diplomacy waslactive, some notion of an out- break was being stimulated in the centre of dis- content. Fuller information may modify these ll4nions, and remove all doubt as to the honour- ale intentions of the Transvaal officials. The situation is still one of considerable seriousness. The Government has made a good many promises 'Without carrying any of them out. The Out- landers are as badly off as ever they were. Mr CHAMBERLAIN'S intervention has done them no good. His scheme of Home Rule has been re- garded with ill-concealed disdain. The conduct of President KRUGER has been callous and almost contemptuous. The dynamite concession, which is alleged to be a breach of the Conventi@n, will probably be revised. Beyond that we cannot see much hope. There is, indeed,a project for a meet- ing between Sir ALFRED MILNER and Presi- dent KRUGER in the Orange Free State in which the idea first assumed a practi- cal shape. Our Government is quite in favour of it, as it ought to be, without having rushed to the conclusion that the negotiating

parties can settle anything by a miscellaneous

_ chat. If there is to be a serious conference, we desire to know upon what programme it is going to be held. What are the proposals upon which President KRUGER is willing to discuss a change in the situation? As the concession will have to came from his side he must make some offer,define Some proposals, or indicate what is to be the basis of the conference. As soon as he has done this, if the terms are such as offer a reasonable hope of acceptance, the conference may be held, and it ought to be of service in bringing a disagreeable and embarrassing situation to a close. Public feeling in this country is in that curious condition in which it can be easily and deeply excited, and might push the Government into grave declara- tions. The end of any tightening of the tension Would be military operations, and it is for Presi- dent KRUGER to make such operations needless by Some timely concessions.

THE Southport election is Protestant regarded as being likely to Signals. exhibit some signals indica- tive of the true feelings of Lancashire, whose Pro- testantism has hitherto been undoubted. There is some stir in the place already, and votes will be east with little regard to anything but the ques- tion now uppermost in men's minds. Lord TIALIFAx is looking forward to the time when the Church shall be supreme in her own affairs, deter- mining doctrine, ritual, and discipline, but retain- ing existing endowments. He claims that tha tnion has been doing splendid work in that direction, and is likely to persist in it until the final triumph is reached. Other persons cannot admit that this kind of freedom will ever be reached without disestablishment and disendow- ruent. Freedom not to be Protestant cannot, we imagine, be obtained in any other way. The hint he has dropped of what the Church Union and the Ritualists intend is a signal for Protestants that they must make up

their minds to two points. Either the Church will have to be internally reformeil upon lines of moderation and oned.nce to the law, or it will have to be disestablislied. The more the difficulties in the way of the former are increased —and they are increashig in several ways—the more we shall be shut up to the latter, as the natural logical and -righteous alternative. The Southport contest seems to be turning upon ecclesiastical politics, Lancashire as a whole is undoubtedly Protestant. It went wrong politi- cally on the point when Mr. GLADSTONE proposed to disestablish the Irish Church. Seat after seat was lost to Om Liberal party because the Lanca shire Protestants feared that permanent injury to the Reformed faith would ensue. It was a mis- take, no doubt, but it has never been repented of, though events have shewn that no danger has occurred, and that the advance of semi-Romanism has been increased by the Conservative legislation of 1674, in favour of which Lancashire M.P.'s were so enthusiastic. The apathy and sympathy of the English Bishops have done ten times more injury to English Protestantism than was ever done by Church disestablishment in Ireland. The admission of this fact is being cheerful:y made at the present moment. It is another Protestant signal of what is coming on. In Southport there ought to be sane strong movement against 7Ritualism, and there is evidence of it already. The Liberal candidate seems likely to have the votes of Protestant clergy and laity, apart from any other matters, because Sir GEORGE PILKING- TOW is sound, and favours legislation, if necessary, to improve the present condition of affairs. As Punch makes it •clear, an apathetic sheep-dog is not of much use. The Bishops cannot do all that is expected of them by those persons who have profited by the inactivity of the Bishops in the past, much less by the active, energetic Protes- tants who desire to see real and not subtle and reserving, obedience in the future. Too much is expected, and there is sure to be corresponding distrust and disappointment. Sonthport election ought to be a danger signal to the Government, as well as to the country at large.

It might have been thought that a Government which has undertaken to play providence to trade could hardly leave office without in some way modifying the glaring deficiencies of the Patent Office; but there is no evidence that any attempt in that direction is to be made. The condition of the law in reference to patents is a public scandal, and the worst of it is that one must charge all Governments, without distinction, with having evaded their responsibility in this matter. What can be said for a system under which a patent is granted and fees are received,and yet no certainty exists that the patent is a patent at all P The authorities at the Patent Office make no examina- tion of records. They simply extort a fee. The aver- age man might then naturally imagine that he had secured immunity on all sides; but in too many instances it is shewn that he has thrown his money away, and he has exposed himself to vexa- tious legal proceedings, and the possibility of be- ing cast in heavy damages into the bargain. Tao patent agents will certainly make an examination of the records—for a large pecuniary consideration —but their opinions are in conclusive. What is wanted is a guarantee from the Patent Office that when a patent has been ("ranted by a mistake, and in ignorance of the fact that a similar patent has previously been granted elsewhere, all fees shall be returned to the owner of the second patent, and he shall be protected from the legal consequences of the error. Such a guarantee would create con- fidence. It is practically given in America. The result is that thousands of British inventors take their schemes to the United States, where they are quickly put into practice, and thus that coun- try is enabled to steal a march upon British manu- facturers, and make inroads into national trade.

Public opinion has conquered—as it was bound to do eventually—and of the two Sabbath-break- ing newspaper proprietors one caved in on Wed- nesday morning, when Mr. Alfred Harmsworth announced the withdrawal of "The Sunday Daily Mail" as a "frank concession to the religictis eling of the public." A similar " concession " on the part of the " Snnday Daily Telegraph" proprietors is necessary before the "None )nformist conscience" will be fully ap- peased. The " Telegraph " people were the prime MOW rs in this innovation which has aroused such widespread antipathy and opposition ; it would have become thorn better had they been the first to acknow- ledge the error of their ways, but their apology for r intruding on the sacredness of the day of rest,must, we believe, come sooner or later. Strong as they are, these newspapers cannot fight against the pub- lic to whom they owe their sustenance. The out- cry which has been raised, and its sequel,are strong testimonies to the sterling worth of the British character, and however much it may be said to have deteriorated we have here ample proof that its quality is still high—higher by far than that of any other nation. It would be interesting to know, by the way, whether the venture bore evi- dences of success as a speculation, or whether the systematic boycotting of the Sunday paper has made its withdrawal a business expediency.

The ebullition of loyalty and aftection towards Her Majesty when at . South Kensington this week was as striking as it was real. Our vener- able and honoured Queen dees not now appear so often in public, and the enthusiasm every- where manifested when she does emerge from her necessary retirement and rest is intense. It falls to the lot of few Sovereigns to win the love of all the subjects of the realm, and Queen Victoria is a striking exception to the generality of rulers in the homage and loyalty which has always been paid her. As years roll on, and her long reign distances all records more and more, so does the concern of the people for their venerable lady's welfare increase, and it is with willingness that they grant her the indul- gence of seldom appearing at public functions how- ever much they may desire the opportunity of welcoming her warmlyand cheering her enthusias- tically.

We have made a rather curious literary find, or rather, we should say, we have found that a certain catch phrase—not a classical expression, but a natural one—in very common use lately, ori- ginally came from the mouth of one of Dickens' characters. Few people, we believe. know from whence the expression" Let e'm all come " origin- ally came. Has it really had any origin wnrthy of note, or has it made its appearance spasmodically? Did it,for instance, make its debut on a music hall stage as the inspiration (?) of a moment? These questions are easy to ask but not easy to answer. However, our readers can verify the fol- lowing for themselves by referring to the pages of "Pickwick Papers." When Mr. Pickwick con- templated engaging Sam as his servant, he asked Mrs. Bardell if, what was good enough to keep one would not suffice for two,and the good woman thought he was proposing to her. The succeed- ing conversation between them only served to make the misunderstanding on the lady's part more complete, and in answer to a remark of Mr. Pickwick's, Mrs. Bardell, with the exclamation "Oh ! you kind, playful dear," flung her arms round his neck and burst into tears. Mr. Pickwick expressed his astonishment at this action and the words, and said,: "Bless my soul! Mrs. Bardell, my good woman—dear me, what a situation—pray consider—Mrs. Bardell—don't—if anybody should come—and it was then Mrs. Bar dell exclaimed frantically "Oh, let e'm all come." Is this the true source of the expression one hears so frequently or is it merely a coincidence ?

We have become accustomed to the phrase that we are a " right little, tight little island," bet it is questionable whether we have fully realised the degree of tightness which exists in London. In a

===== 0002601_18990519_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
TIM EXAMINER, FRIDAY MAT 19, 1899. RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

strikineseries of articles upon, the overcrowding of London which have been contributed by Mr. George Haw, to the "Daily News "the veil has been lifted somewhat, and w are the better able to appreciate the difficulties of the seething mass of people in the Capital to fild a place to shelter in. Here is a sad picture of what is taking place in the Inner Bolt, lying between the City and the suburbs:—"Houses and tenements and one roomed houses have been divide I and sub-dlvided till now it has come to pass that the veky beds are let oft at so much rent. It is becoming common for night- workers, such as bakers and watchmen, to rent a be. to sleep in during the day from people already living in an overcrowded state who occupy the bed at night. Among soine families in Spitalfields the beds are rented on the eight hour principle,having three different sets of sleepers every twenty-four hours." It would be interesting to know whether these are advertised on the familiar card so often displayed in lodging-houses "Well aired beds." Cases of overcrowding have been reported in Warrington, but the evil is as no- thing compared with what is daily taking ' place in London. At the last census there were three thousand people living eight or more in a room, over nine thousand living seven or more in a room, and at the lowest twenty-six thousand are living six or more in a room. It is not surprising to learn that this overcrowding causes loss of home life, immorality, drunkenness, debility, consumption, disease, and unnecessary deaths, or that it debases the boys and ruins the girls. Such a painful state of things is a sad com- mentary on our inability to cope successfully with the ever-increasing difficulty of the aggregation of our people in huge centres.

It is indeed strange that the responses to the majority of appeals for subscriptions in aid of a Gladstone memorial in various towns and cities have been so poor. No man was ever more de- serving of having his name honoured and his memory kept green than Mr.Gladstone,and we are convinced that nine cut of every ten persons would frankly admit this. Yet they are phenorninally slow in giving practical testimony to their appre- ciation of the dead statesman. Even Liverpool, with its reputation for generosity and the high honnur it acquires through Mr. Gladstone, in that he was born in the port, has been very tight-pocketed over its Gladstone memorial, and though the fund closes on Saturday only sufficient has been contributed to provide for a decent statue. A much more ambitious memorial scheme was originally formulated, and it will be the greatest of pities and a lasting reflection on the opulent citizens of the Grand Old Mm's birth- place, if it has to be greatly cut dowa owing to paucity of subscriptions. We hope the Liver- pudlians will rally during the coming week and see that a memorial is raised which will do honour to him whom it commemorates, and to the city which claims him a% her most illustrious son.

There is much to be said for the contention of the "Times of India" that the campaign against foreign seamen in the British mercantile marine should not be pressed with special severity against the Lascar. Apparently, Indian opinion has put an unfavourable construction upon recent developments of the manning question, and we are reminded that under the British flag all the Queen's subjects are supposed, so far as possible, to have a claim for equal treatment,irrespeetive of colour or creed. Unfortunately, the Indian pub- licists do not attempt to deal with that part of the problem which most closely interests the ocean traveller. Lasear seamen, while ousting British labour from British ships,do not create the same confidence among the passengets. At critical moments they ureak away from discipline, and in- crease both the confusion and the danger for all concerned. There may be no worthier community among the Indian races than these marines,and it is certain some of them must always be employed in vessels that ply between temperate and torrid latitudes ; but there are serious arguments against the policy of shipping them wholesale. If they are permitted to undersell British seamen, there is no longer that equality of treatment for which Indian public opinion clamours.

RUNCORN NOTES,

tBY A CORRESPONDENT ] It is evident that there exists dissatisfaction in some quarters when we see three persons nominated for the post of secretary DO the Football Club. It is certainly a post of honour—if not the post of honour —and requires for the discharge of its duties more than a narrow officialism, that cannot expand to meet all the requiremcints and exigencies which ac- compsny the management f a football club. The successful secretary must have the " bel esprit of Napoleon and the patience of a Job " before he can hope to please everyone.

The tlaree names submitted will be : Messrs. J. FCllisou (the retiring secretary), Alfred Abel,and W. Jones. As things appear at present, in certain quarters a change from the old order is being ad- vocated. But from what I have seen of Mr. J. Ellison, if be is not the beau ideal he is certainly a painstaking secretary. The real difficulty of the " opposition" will be to get the other men to stand; but if they do Mr. Ellison may suffer defeat from the " progressive " party. As all are highly capable men, luckily the choice can- not greatly affect the status of the club.

The unopposed re-electioa of the 'ovular presi- dont Mr. P. Speakman, C.C., and the treasurer, Mr. C. Pownall, show how firmly the affections of the club members are wound around those names. The ,other nominations are eminently satisfactory and however the polling results good and true men will be secured for the executive.

The meeting of the Halton Parish Council, on Wednesday evening was devoid of anything of public interest. Mr. Mannering presided over a small attendance.—Some controversy exists as to the trusteeship of the Halton Charities, but the official intimation from the Charity C3mmissioners to the effect that Sir Richard Brooke remains the trustee, should end it. Matters are practically as they were. It is satisfactory to be able to observe it is so; and gratifying to know that the whims of a Parish Council cannot upset what has hitherto been satisfactory.

An amusing, though sad incident was that which came before the Runcorn Bench on Wednesday and Thursday mornings. Margaret Finn prosecuted Thomas Finn for the larceny of a pair of boots valued at 43 lid. But feeling unable to conduct the prosecution unaided, she had recourse to a supply of "Dutch courage ", with the consequence that the presiding magistrate Mr. J. Ockleston ordered her to ha confined in the cells until the next day. On the morrow, however, after successfully sustaining her prosecution, the woman was herself despatched to prison for 7 days.

The Whitsuntide holidays will be observed in the usual way at Runcorn. Already all the Sunday schools have made their arrangements for the usual procession and treat. There is, however, a lament- able decrease in the number of clubs that will observe what has from time immemorial been a general annual Walking Day. The discontinuance of this feature of the local Whit-Monday observ- ances is generally regretted. On Tuesday the town will present an almost deserted appearance. For the first time the Volunteers will encamp during Whit-week. The camp will be at Blackpool, and no doubt many will avail themselves of the oppor- tunity of going to Blackpool to see the men gathered under canvas.

HOSPITAL EXTENSION AT WIDNES.

On Tuesday the foundation stone of the new exten- sion of the Widnes Infectious Diseases Hospital was laid by Councillor George Niel, chairman of toe Health Comnaittee,in the presence of the Mayor (Alderman Vareing) wad toe members of the Council. The new extension will provide accomoaodation for matron and nurses and also for eleven mile and eleven female patien:e. The total cost, incladiog the land, will amour t to £9,340. The Mayor presented Councillor Niel with a handsome silver trowel, appropriately inscribed, and an ebony mallet. Councillor Niel,in perfornaiog the ceremony, said when the building was completed they would be able to make provision for almoss any ordin- ary epidemic which m grit occur in the borouga. After the usual votes of tnaolcs the company drive to the fowa Hali.where they were entertained to luncheon by Councillor Niel. A RuNcoaN Mtol's ESCAPADE AT SHREWSBURY.

At the Snrewsbury Borough Police Court on Saturday James Rollason, painter, Runcorn, was brought up in custoly charged vv.th committing wilful damage to cells at the police station. Prisoner behaved in a most extra- ordinary fashions. He appeared in court with his coat tied round his shoulders, and he had a nasty viound on the side of his face. When askel to plead he refuse.d to speak, simply nodding and shaking his bead, teough the Chief Ornstsble stated that he c ould talk well enough. The evidence was that on the previous night prisoner was found lying in the street drunk, and was removed to the police station. On being placed in the cell he be- haved in the most violent manner. He pulled all the woo iwork down, and broke it to pieces, practically des- troying everything inside. Two officers went to him to rPin ova his boots, when a desperate struggle took place. He was at length overpowered and removed to another cell, where he renewed his violent conduct, tearing up his blanket and any clothing which came in his way. Prisoner was remanded. A PROMISING CAREER CUT SNORT. It is with regret that we record this week the death of Thomas klfred Millinger, the second so of Captain A. Millinger, of Trentham-street, Runcorn, who passed away on Wednesday afternoon, after a prolonged illness at the age of 22 years. A little more than three months ago he ruptured a blood vessel and suffered greatly from internal heernorrhage of the lungs, which ultimately developed into a rapid consumption and culminated in his death on Wednesday as stated. Though so young Mr. Millinger was a general favourite though- out the town ; and especially amongst the students at the Technical Institute, who greatly esteemed him. His klowledge of foreign languages was extensive, and he was reputed to be able to speak French like a native. For years he has been employed at the H.zlehurst Soapery as clerk, and his know- ledge of languages and general business cap- abilites marked him out as one who was destined to figure out a career of exceptional attainments. me regret at his untimely demise is manifest on all hands but more especially his aged parent feels his loss as irreparable. The youth of the town mourn his loss as the loss of a friend, the aged will miss his cheery word and kindly greeting and numbers silently testify to a blank that cannot be filled. With his aged father, his brothers and relatives a sympathic townsfolk mourn that a career of such promise should have been so abruptly terminated. The funeral takes place at 1.30 on Sunday next. MYSTERIOUS DROWNING CASE AT WIDNES.

On Saturday, at the Widnes Aecideut Hospital, Mr: ' S. Brighouse, eouoty coroner, held an inquest upon the body of a man unknown, who was found drowned in the Widnes and Sankey Canal on Friday.—Thomas Stokes, captain of the flat Addle, living at 7, Stoke- street, Runeora, said that he found the body in the Sankey Can ii, on Friday, about 10-30 a. m ,opposite the Alkali Works, and he sent information to the police. The body was clothed with the exception of a shoe and hat. It must have been in the water for a long time,probably about a fortnight.—P.S. Williams said they received in- formation from Stokes, that while he was unloading his boat they found the body coming from the direction of Cuerdley. With the assistance of Stokes and a man named Hill, witness and P.C. Noar removed the body to the mortuary of the hospital. The body wag that of a man about 20 years of age, height sfl 21n with brown hair, no moustache, and clothed in a black serge coat and vest, cotton shirt, grey woollen singlet (no stockinga), one" Blucher " boot fastened with a clasp, two pairs of trousers, one cord, the other striped. De- ceased appeared to be a labouring man, and had in his pocket a small black leather parse.but there was nothing to state who he was. Several persons had seen him, but had failed to identify him.—ln reply to the Coroner, witness said the body hadi not been photographed, as it was too decomposed.—A. juryman said he could not see why it should not be photographed.—The Coroner said he thought it should be photographed. He had been rather surprised to hear the first witness state the body hod been in the water a hag time. He had seen the body, and he did not share the same opinion. It had been through his representation that the C)unty Coroners had adopted the photographing of unidenti- fied bodies. It sometimes happined that bodies had been identified even months after the inquest. The Coroner promised to view the body aeaia, and give in- structions a 4 to photographing it.—The jury returned a verdict of "Found drowned."

RUNCORN VOLUNTEERS AT CHESTER. -CA3fP ARRANGE- MENTS

On Saturday last the F a,nd G Companies of the Run- corn Detachment of the 201 B ttt. E. Chester Volun- teers, under the command of Captain G. F. Ashton, left Runcorn with the 2.25 p.m. train for Chester. There were about 114 non-commissioned officers and mon on parade, and these were joined by 90 of the Frodaham Company. Upon arriving at Che-ter they were joined by the Chester Companies and marched round to North- gate Station to meet the Weaverham Companies, which brought the number up to about 450. It was so wet that it was decided to alter the arrangements and drill in the Castle yard instead of the Roodee. Several bat- talion movements were attempted but the continual downpour of rain interferred with their effectiveness. After several line and column movements and manual exercise, the men were marched to the 111De3 Stands on din Roodee where refresh- ments were supplied to them, and proceeding from thence to the Drill Hall, Chester, were dismissed after being complimented on Lae esprit de corps they ex- hibifepl. The battalion drill was a preliminary to the Brigade Camp, at Blackpool, which commences on Saturday next, to proceed to which the Runcorn De- tachment will parade at the Drill Hall, at 545 p.m., in full marching order. The inspection day will be on Wednesday. This year will be tbe first year that the Cheshire battalions have been brigaded, and should be productive of keener competition amongst ;he men. In former years the Cheshire battalions used to brigade with the Welsh border companies.

THE TRAP ACCIDENT NEAR RUNCORN.

FATAL SEQUEL. INQUEST AND VERDICT.

_ At"the Police Court, Runc3rn, on Wednesday morning, Mr. J. E. worslay, coroner, held an inquest uoon the body of Mrs. Margaret Acton, widlor, aged 68 years, who died on Sunday last, May 14th, from the effects of injuries recivel in a trap accident on the 23rd August last.—The foreman of the jury was Mr. D. B. Jones.— Considerable interest was manifested in the procee hinge as the facts of the cage lilvo already- been the subject of legal proceedings, at Runcorn County Court, and the Liverpool Asgizge. Walter Gerrard, 35, Waterloo-road, Runcorn, commis- sion agent, said deceased was his mother-in-law. She was 68 years old last November. In July she suffered from hemorrhage, and was attended by Dr. Orntsby. She fully recovered from that On the 23rd of August

there was an accident to a trap. Fle was driving a pony and a trap. The deceased and his two little boys and a boy namel Leonard Pye were with him. He was driv- ing round Aston, and when coming round Aston-lane past Sutton, on nearing Sutton Hall Farm, he noticed some distance in front of them a milk float, occupied by two young men. The float was coming on the same side of the road as that which he occupied but from the op- posite direction. The occupants of the float did not seem to take any notice so witness called out "Eh up there," twice. By that time the vehicles had come nearer and he noticed one of the men lean for- ward, take up his reins and commence to pull his horse to the other side of the road. In order to allow float as much time and room as possible so that it could get past all right,witaess stopped his horse and trap. It would be about a foot off the ditch at the time. Just, however, as the float was passing the man stooped and took up a stick with which he struck the celt on the left side. Tbe colt reared up and some of the tankards fell out. The horse belted forward, coming towards the right with the result that the right wheel of the milk float collided with the nave of the right wheel of witness's trap, breaking both the shafts. The occupants were pitched out with the exception of a little boy aged 4. The de- ceased was much hurt, and sustained a scar down the left side of her face; the inside of her mouth was cut and bleeding; her shoulder was hurt,as well as the back of her neck and other minor injuries. She was not. however, insensible. Dr. Ormsby was called in to see her the same night —The Coroner: What has been her state of health since P—Wi`ness: She never recovered in the least. She has never been able to dress herself. Never left her bed the whole time.—The Coronert: Was this accident the subject of jegal proceedings P— Witness: My brother brought an action for darnagei to the trap and obtained a verdict for the full amonnt of damage to trap, pony, and harness.—The Coroner: There was an action at the Asaizes.—Witness : An action was tried at the Liverpool Assizes for personal injuries sustained and the result of the action was a verdict for the plaintiff.—The Coroner : Upon what cause ?--Witness : That I can- not say, sir.—The Coroner Did the jury state the circumstances? Witness : They did not give any ex- pression of opinion sir.—Continuirag witne.s said de- ceived had never been the same since the day of the accident, and her clothes had to he cut off her as they could not undress her. At the time of the accident de- ceased was convalescent and intended going away for a change, and had the evident not happened she would have gone away. He did not think that the driver of the float was sober at the time.

thel

Dr. George Henry Ormsby, Runeorn, stated he had attended the deceased from the 4th of July last. Previ- ous to the time he had attended her, hut he was not quite clear on the point, for something of a slight nature. On the 4th of July he was called in to attend her, and found her to be suffering from hemorrhage. He might explain that the hemorrhage she was suffering from need not necessarily have been cerlebral hemorrhage. It may, have been only a clot of blood that got on to the brain and a very small clot at that, though they could only judge from the symptoms exhibiied. Mrs. Acton recovered from that attack with the excention of feeling a little weak. He kad attended her almost continually from the 4th, to the 30th of July. On the 23rd of August be was called in to her after the trap accident ani found her suffering as the last witness had described. It was not exactly a scar on the side of the face, but a bruise. The wounds were superficial, but the effect was principally shock. In his opinion she died from shock consequent upon the injuries received. She died on the 14th inst and the cause of death would be "nervous prostration." When witness first knew Mrs. Acton she was a active woman both mentally and physically and full of vitality; but after the accident she could not do anything for her- self.

The Coroner, in summing up, said it was for the jury to decide whether they thought there was sufficient cause for them to adjourn the inquirrlf they considered there was so much bl,rne to be attached to any person as to amount to manslaughter he would abjeurn the in- quiry and they could have the driver and other wit- nesses present, and he would go into the cause of the accident. Oa the other hand he would not advise them to that course, and considered that the acount of the accident as related by the son-in-law afforded sufficient grounds for them to sta"e that death was evidently caused by " nervous prostration consequent on the re- sult of the trap accident on the 23rd of August." The jury after a short adjournment brought in a ver- dict of "Death from nervons prostration, consequent on the trap accident on the 23rd of August."

A SAILOR'S BODY FOUND IN THE CANAL.

Ur. J. E. Wor3ley, coroner, held an inquest, at the Weaver Hotel, ‘Vesron Point, on Fiday m)rning, upon the body of Richard Evans' aged 54, of Malire, Angle- soy, whose body was found floating in the Runcorn and Weston Canal on the previous Wedni-sday.—Mr. Hugh Evans, Malfre, Anglesey, identified the body as that of his brother Richard, aged 54 years, who was a sailor on board the " Sarah Lloyd" of Bangor.—James Street, lock-tenter, of Dukes Fields, Ruicara, gave evidence of seeing the deceased on the 29th of April at a quarter past seven. He was sober then and came to witness's cabin for a " pipe-light." Witness seeing he was a stranger asked if he was going to R mcorn and he replied" I am going to Weston Point to have a glass of beer, after which I will turn in.—Superintendent Pear- son, submit'ed the statements of William Morris, a.b , of the steamship" Avon," and of Owen Jones, captain of the" Sarah Lloyd," which showed that deceased had visited Morris on tne 29. h of April on the" Avon," and left there at 11 o'clock in a ItobPr condition. Captain Jones' statement was of a nature to corriborate the above, and also contained a description of the deceased whom he reported missing on the 30:h of April.—The Coroner read both statements, but observed they could not be. 'taken as part of the evidence.—Join 1,100; of Weston Point, labourer, stated he was told there was a body in the canal, and getting assistance recovered it about 12 o'clock on the 10th inst. He should think that the body was recovered about 200 yards from the spot where the ship lay whilst at the cicck.—P.C. George Bancroft, who be- longed to the St. J )bn Anal:mimic said he examined the body. There were no marks of violence upon it. The underclothing was marked with " RE," and the general description answered to that circulated of the miQsing sailor Richard Evans. In the pockets he found 3s 3ii and a knife. He had made enquiries, but no one remembered seeing deceased, or anyone of his descrip- tion, and so far as he could ascertain, he had not ben drinking anywhere. The night of the 29th was a very dark night, and as there were no lights in that part of the canal, he would give it as his opinion the deceased may have missed his way in going from one ship to the other, and walked into the canal.—The Coroner in sum- ming up, advised the jury to bring in a verdict of "found drowned." He remarked that it was a lament- able occurrencv. The man was evidently a sober man who had missed his way, and walked into the canal.— The jury agreed.

CYCLING.

I have lately been making a change in chains. My old bin& chain having becomo a little worn I cast shout for a successor, and necided upon the Alfred Arnaleby twin-roller chain as the one most sulable. This iP my first expsriericc of roller ci,ains and I must say at cane that the chain I have now in we is a great improvement on the previous one. A block chain always OVFS satisfac- tion, if it is a good make, but for sweet running it cannot compare with the half•inch pitch el. twin-roller. NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Authors of letters intended for publication must in at eases send their names and addre4ses to the Editor. 'Meters intend, d for publication must be written on one side only of the piper. Correspondents are strongly advised to write briefly. A short letter has much greater chance of insertion than a long one.

THE EXPLOSION AT ST. HELENS. Sir,—ln conwiction with more than one scientific problem, it would be of interest to know how far the disastrous explosion at St. Hee is last Fridiy was felt and heard ; and I should be greatly obliged to any of your vaderswho would be sn kind as to send mri a postcard stating ; 1, the namn of the place. and the time, at which the explosion was observed ; 2, whether any sh,ick or vibration was bit; 3, whether the sound ot the explosion Wes heard; and 4, wheth9r the vibrations wore strong enough to make windows rattle.—Yours obediently,

CHARLES DAVISON. Sc. D., F.G.S. 16, Manor-road, Birmingham, May 13.
