ST. PAUL'S CHURCH'CUOIR

Sir,—l shall be much Obliged if you will allow me a little of your valuable space in your paper to advocate an Improvement in the singing, &c. of St. Paul's choir. Everyone knows of the good name they have held in the past for their singing but after last Sunday niglr's performance I mug say that it has altered very much for the worse. I have been connected with the church for the pa,t 19 years but yet never did I hear an anthem so wretchedly rendered as the one they rendered last Sunday and I am sure I have heard it sung by them almost a score of times. The organ has an idea that it ought to be heard and not the choir in almost every- thing, and especially if there happens to be a solo to be sung. In fact things have gene altogether backward; of late. No doubt they would do better if they could get their old principal tenor back again to keep things in order.—Your3 &c.,

SIDESMAN.

STOCKTON HEATH SWING BRIDGE. Sir,—May I through the medium of your paper call the attention of the powers that be, to the danger. arising from the ineffectual lighting of the above bridges At the beat it is very poor, but for the last we 4 or so. the lamp on the Warrington silo has been out more ofren than in. The lamp on the Stockton Heath side has also lately been out. I atn told that the Stockton Heath lamp is turned out (when it happens to be in) at twelve 'clock along with the other lights in the Village and that there is something the matter with the Warring- ton one. Surely this wants looking into and I hope that the Corporation and the Stockton Heath Parish Council will see that good Brays lamps are put up at each side and that they are kept lighted thrcughout the night otherwise a anions acci- wilisurely occur. I have seen many " near shavr,s," myself,there,particularly when the bridge has been open for a passing ship and the traffic on the road Las accu- mulated in consequence. I do not know whether the Ship Canal Company are liable for the lighting but if so, they ought not to be spared is:the matter. They them- selves have nct studied the public in leling the ap- proaches,for the unsightly hoardings which are being erected at each bridge. Trusting the a forementio7ed bodies will bestir them- selves before it is tan late, and an accilent has oecured, I remain, yours,

A HEATHEN.

THE SABBATH: SECULAR SIDE. Sir,—The new-born zeal that is now manifesting itself in tone religious quarters regarding the Day of Rest is a strange phenomenon, and has come too late in the day to be of any practical value. If a strict observance of the secular &de of the Sabbath had been faithfully preached and pressed home on the people in the past, we should not have to deplore the alarming extent to which its vielation prevails at present. But, for reasons only known ts themselves, our religious mentors seem to give more attention to the souls, than to the bodies of men, although bath are equally precious in the sight of God. Besides, does it not leek very like a tarce to see them going about the country denouncing and boycotting Sunday labour in a few printing and publishine estehlichments while the Post Office, Carriage and Railway Compenies,,ane Public Reuses are permitted and encouraeed to carry on their operations on Sundays without protest or hindrance of any kind. The agitation got up by the Bishops and Free Chur:h Councils is too ill-timed and absurd to merit serious criticism. The law which authorisei and regulates the Day of Rest is not a human, but a divine institution, and is as old as the world itself. In fact it carries US back to the time wnen " God blessed the seventh clay, and sanctified it : because that in it he had rested from all his work which God created and made." (G n. 11., 3 ) Its rehearsal to Israel in the Wilderness was only the reiteration of a principle th :t had been in force from the beginning' The word" Remember" with which the Fourth Com- mandment opens confirms this view. And further testi- mony on this pint is afforded by the division of time into periods cf seven days by the ancient Egyptians, Greeks and Romanis, and even by the origins' peoplesof ' the new world. Indeed it may be fairly aesumed that , wherever the idea of worship has prevailed—and when and where has it n,t prevailed in the eistory of man- kind—there most have been appointed times when men laid aside their usual occupations that they might, quietly and without distraction, worthies the Great Spirit in accordance with the light he had given them. Nor has God left US in any doubt as to the meaning of the earthly side of the law lam considering. The language in which it is conveyed is of the simplest kind, terse, and to the point, and easy of comprehension by the poorest and most unlearned amongst us. Briefly summarised, the Sabbath Law absolutely prohibits every kind of labour by men and animals on the seventh day. There is not a word,not a phrase in the Command- ment that will bear may other interpretation. The keen- est scrutiny looks in vain to it for anything to counten- ance the labours and amusements which so much die. figure the "Poor Man's Day" in our time. The reader can easily satisfy himself on this point by consulting Exodus xx., 8,9, 10, 11. Further, the law of a weekly rest day, like the law of labour, demands nothing less than universal obedience. When God doomed Adam and his posterity to labour for their daily bread he made no provision for idleness, tin arraneemsnt whereby some members of the commu- nity might live at ease while their poorer brethren toiled in the sweat of their brow. With him there is no reseect of persons, and no class legislation. The prince and the peasant are equal in his sight as regards their a ogation to obey his behest's. Asd the same divine im- partiality distinguishes the law of the Sabbath. Abso- life rest from all worldly employments is the very essence of that law. It admits of no modifications to the views of men, and no one, while resting himself, has any right to exact labour. from his poorer brethren on the Sabbath. The Omniscient framer of the Fourth Commandment knew that all the legitimate and useful work of the world could be performei insix days a week. And he knew further that that work would entail hard toil and suffering upoa the great bulk of mankind, and eo to lighten and brighten their lot he mercifully gave them one day in seven to rest from all their labours under the sun. And the same mercy he.extended to the dumb animals. They too are entitled to e tire cessation from all work on toe seventn day, and that by Divine appointment. And lastly.the law of the Sabbath being a lac of God, it is not sutl,ct to abrogation or revision by men So Lone as the heavens end the earth remain to eemind us of its institutioreane so long as lost is needed by men so long will that Law c mtinue in force.—Yours truly, lONA.

GOOD TIMPLARY. The usual weekly session of the Temperance Mission Lodge of the 1.0.G.T. was held on Wednes- day in the Temperance Mission Room, Academy street. Bro Betties, C.T., presided over a good attendance. Bro Roberts,secretary, read the digest of the Smith West Lancat.hire District Lodge to be held at Southport on Saturday next. The report of the Ne. 1 Convention meeting, which was held last Saturday at Athercon, was read and a discussion followed which was taken part in by Brothers O'Brien, Betties, R. obprts, J Sheath, and Nurse. It was decided to invite the Hope of Barlestown lodge on Wednesday next, the lodge to be open to the public at 8-30. The lodge was closed in the usual manner by Bro Betties, C.T.

EARLESTOWN & NEWTON.

POLICE CASES.

At the Warrington Occasional Court on Tugsday morning before Mr. James Tinsley, Frank Whitting- ham a bricksetter of Winwick was charged with being drunk and disorderly in Warrington-road, Winwick on the afternoon of Monday. He was shouting, cursing and making a great noise and P.C. Groarke took him into custody. Ile was fined 22 6d and Costs. TIA.YDOCK MA.Y QUEEN FESTIVAL. The celebration of the Crowning of the May Queen which was being resuscitated this year in Haydock, had to be abandoned on Saturday in con- sequence of the heavy downpour of rain. 'this was a great disappointment to hundreds of children and I necesparily entailed a considerable loss to the com- mittee who bad the affair in hand. It was hoped at noon that matters might improve, but as these hopes were not realised it was finally decided to postpone the event for a fortnight. NEWTON FAIR.

The annual May fair for the sale of horses and cows, was held at Newton on Wednesday. Judging from the attendance, it would seem as if the New- ton fair, which at one time wa4; so popular, is gradu- ally waning in importance. There was a very poor show of cows on Wednesday, but of horses a very fair number was put on the market. These chiefly consisted of heavy draught horses, and moderate prices were obtained. Buyers, however, were not numerous, and the business transacted was not of the best.

===== 0002601_18990519_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
GOOD TEMPLARY.

The usual weekly meeting of the Hope of Earles- town lodge of Good Templars was held in the Co- operative Hall, Wargrave-road, on Monday evening. The meeting was opened by Bro. Newman, S.J.T. Bro. Woods submitted a report of the Cmvention held at Alleston on the 13h inst. Arrangements were being made to hold open air meetings in the neighbourhood, and the programme committee ap- pointed was Bro. Woods and Leatherbarrow, Sisters Williams and Walton. The programme for the evening consisted of a reading by Bro Newman, songs by Bro. Singleton,Sisters Woods,and Thomas; reading, Bro Anderton. The lodge was closed in the usual form.

THE WHITSUNTIDE HOLIDAYS AT EA.RLESTOWN. I A complete change will very likely be made in connection with the observance of Whirsiintile, in Earlestown. It has been customary for Whit- Monday to be observed as a general holiday, and on this day, all !he Sunday schools hold a demonstra- tion and field day. All the works were closed, and the business people also closed their premises. This year however, it has been decided to close the Via- duct Works for three days, and it was left to the workmen to say whether they would have the first or last half of the work. On a canvas being made, the men decided by a very large majority, to take the last three days of the week as a holiday, and consequently the works will close on Thursday Friday and Saturday next week. The Vulcan Foundry will not close at all during Whit week, but will play a week in August. Messrs. T. and J. Vicars, and Messrs. Slee and Co., will close on Whit- Monday, so that it is evid.-nt the general holiday is likely to become a thing of the past.

THE WHITSUNTIDE DEMONSTRATIONS AT EARLESTOWN.

A meeting of the committee who have in hand the arrangements in cpnnection with the Sunday School Demonstration annually held on Whit Monday, met on Wednesday evening in the Lecture Hall of the Wesleyan Church, under the presidency of Mr. Peter Parr. The meeting became necessary in consequence of the alteration in the holidays, brought about by the abandonment of New- ton Races. It has always been the rule to close the various works on Whit 'Monday, and as everybody was then free the Sunday school demonstrations and field days were held. Tnis year the Viaduct works employes have decided to have their holiday on the last three days of next week and this decision has upset all the arrangements of the schools. It was thought that the procession could have been held on Thursday next, but the bands which had been engaged could not give their services on that day. Conz,equently, after discussing the matter in all its bearings, the committee de- cided to postpone the demonstration and field day until either the 3rd or 10th of June,whichever day will be suitable to the engaged bands. EARLESTOWN CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY.

The 154th quarterly meeting of this society was held in the Co-operative Hall on Monday evening. Mr. D. Forrester presided and there was a large at- tendance of members. The report and balance sheet showed that the cash receipts for the quarter were 1:8413 16s 1011 being an increase over laQt quarter of £llO7 16s 11d, and £2383 13s 6,1- over the c Tres ponding quarter last year. The nett profits realised were £1276 13s 7d out of which a dividend of 3s in the round was recommended to members, requiring £12473s 51, the balance of £29 103 id, being de- voted to the reduction of fixed stock. During the quarter 81 new members had been admitted and 8 had withdrawn leaving 1,400 on the books, 1055 of whom had brought in checks. The minutes of the last quarterly meeting were real and confirmed and it was stated that the question of telephonic com- munication with the branch shops had been aban- doned for the present. Messrs. E. Jones and T. Swift were appoiated to serve on the Committee of Management. Mr. J. Jones was re-elected stock- taker. The usual annual grant was made in con- nection with the employes annual trip. Aft appli- cation for a grant from the local Company of the let V B.S. Lancashire Regiment towards their prize fund was brought up and after some discussion, it was decieed not to entertain it. An application from members of the Seciety who hold cottages unler mortgage to the Society, for a reduction in the rate of interest was also not entertained. The question of remuneration to the Sub-committee in connection with the building of the branch shows was referred to the General Committee for settle- ment. This was all the business. EARLESTOWN WESLEYAN SUNDAY SCHOOL. In connection with the anniversary of the above school, special services were held in the chapel on Sunday. Great preparations are made for these services, and there are invariably crowded congre- gations at each. The weathenon Sunday,however, was not of the best,but this did not militate against the attendance, although the children suffered some inconvenience because of their dresses. The preacher for the day was the Rev. A. Percy Watson of Bradford, and formerly a resident minister at I Earlestown. Special hymns were sung at the morn- ing and evening services, a choir of children having been specially trained by Miss Greenall, the organ- ist. This choir was seated on a temporary gallery and very nice the children looked in their charming dresses. At the morning service Miss Olive Clare sang as a solo the hymn "In a world where sor- row," the cho;r of children taking up the chorus. The Rev. A. Watson preached an instructive sermon from the 11th chapter Deuteronomy, and part of 21 verse. At the evening service the chapel was crowded, and many were unable to ob- tain admittance. Miss M. Barr sang as a solo " I will not go where I cannot take Jesus," tiss L. Clarke sang" It's coming. coming nearer," and -bliss May Clare rendered the solo "Abide with me." OPENING OF THE INQUEST,

Notwithstanding the inclemency of the weather on Saturday,the terrible explosion which took place on Friday morning at Kurtz's works of the United Alkali Company, St. Helens, attracted to the town many thousands of people for the purpose of seeing the scene of the disaster. Another death took place shortly before nine o'clock in the St. Helens Hospital, thus making flue deaths in all. The last death was that of John Grey, a fireman at Kurtz's, who was sixty-four years of age, and resided in Manor-street. There were in the hospital five men, two youths, and four women. The men and one woman were in a very critical condition, but the rest of the number were progressing as well as could be expected under the circum,tances. The last patient admitted was a Mrs. Costello, who re- sides in Walker-lane near the scene of the explo- sion, and who was suffering trom severe vitriol scalds and other injuries. The excitement which had been aroused in St. Helens and district has now greatly subsided,thougn the subject is still the leading topic of conversation on every hand. All traces of the destruction which was wrought have been gradually disappearing from the main thoroughfares of the town, and things have almost reached their normal aspect- though in some instances tradesmen have not yet been able to have the damage to their premises re- paired so great has been the demand upon the ser- vices of plumbers, glaziers, Btc. In the Yeasley Cross district,however,the wrecked cottages remain to tell of the terrific forces woich had thrown death and destruction around the seat of the explosion. The comity coroner formally opened an inquiry in- to the circumstances surrounding the death of the unfortunate victims, but before any evidence upon tbat question was tendered the proceedings were adjourned until the 30th inst. The jurors and other interested persons subsequently paid a visit to the works, and minutely inspected the scene of the ex- plosion. They were accompanied by Mr. Stewart the general manager; Mr. Rudge, manager of the works; Inspector Hunter and Sergeant BissOn. Mr. Stewart and Mr. Rude carried out, for their en- lightenment, several excerimtnts, such as trying to explode the chlorate by concussion and then by fire.

Colonel Ford, the chief explosive inspector for the Home Office, visited St. Helens on Monday for the purpose of making an inspection of the scene of the disaster, and had an interview with Mr. Brig- house, the county coroner. Colonel Ford, stated that, if necessary, he will bring Dr. Dupre, analy- tical expert, to attend the full inquiry. OPENING OF THE INQUEST. SYMPATHY FOR CRE BEREAVED AND SUFFERERS. _ .

CoMPENSAifION CLAIMS.

Since the inquest proceedings on the five victims of the terrible explosion at Kurtz' s Chemical Works, St. Hel9ns, Mr H L Riley, solicitor, who appeared on beh If of the relatives of three of the deceased men, has com- municated with Messrs Forshaw mad Hawkins, solicitors for the United Alkali Company, Limited, suggesting that in cases of fatal injury to worknen of the company the result nf the inquest could in no way effect the com- pany's liability to pay compsnsatiora under the Workmen's Compensation Act. Messrs Forshaw and Hawkins have replied that they agree with Mr. Riley's view, giving him the name of the company with whom they were insured under the Act named, and stating that they had instructed their local manager to supply full particulars of the past earnings of the various deceased, in order ta facilitate the computation of the amounts to which their dependents might be entitled.

The Plate Glass Insurance Company, of London, who have a large number of policies in St. Helens, and suffered considerably through the explotion, notwith- standing their non-liability for accident from explosion, have replaced all their claims as an act of grace through their local agents, Messrs J. B. and B. Leach,Hardshaw- street, St. Helens. In this course ef action the com- pany are following the policy of the St. Helens and Dis trict Mutual Plate Glass Insurance C impany, the Chair- man of which is Citricillor U. Martin, and the secretary Mr. G. C. Whitfield, Tne Mutual Comp any never made any attempt to repudian their liability to their policy holders, but proceeded with the u ;most diligence to make replacements. In this matter they had been act- ing under the advice of their solicitor, Mr. H. L. Riley, who had given notice on their behalf to the United Alkali Company, Limited ,that they would se3k to be in- demnified by them for the whole of th?,ir outlay.

STATEMENT BY THE CHAIRMAN OF THE FINANCE COMMITTEE.

Mr. Orntand (chairman of the Finance Committee' said he should like to rsfer to the financial position oi Council riot only for the year ending 31,t March last, but for the financial year they had just entered upon, ending 3lat March, 1900. Oa the General Improvement Fund eccoant, the actual income was £3,944 which was within £5 of the estimate. Tne expenditure was £4,374 or £430 more than the income. There were several contributory causes to that increased expendirure, chief of wnich he might mention an anticipated thficit of £ll6. That they knew atiout. There must also!he ad led the cost of the storm ovn-fl in W argrave-road which amounted to the sum of £9B and for which no provision was made in the rate levied twelve months ago. No provision was made alno for the alterations and repairs to the property which the Coun- cil purchased from Mr. S tnderson and which had cost £5O. The cost of sewage disposal had also exceeded their estimate by £62, and the night-to.' by £llB, of which £7O went in wages. If they added these items together they would see that £445 hal been explained so that if they had not had these extra or special items they would have had a decreased rather than an increased expenditure. There were only £24 uncollected which was the lowest amount that had ever been outstanding so far as he had been aele to trace. (Hear, hear.) On the Hiehways Fund account the estimated income was £2,824 and the expenditure £2687, leaving a balance of £137 over expend,ture. This was very easily explained becaute the road roller which wts let out on hire to other authorities produced more than was anticipated. The income from this source must be of a fugitive character bocause they did not know to what extent it would be required by other local authoritiet. Tiler° had also been a less sum spent on roads other than main or secondary roads. The amount of rate uncollected on this account was £l4 as compared with £l5 and as against £254 only ten years ago. On the water rate account, the "ad- mated income was £707, and the expenditure £721, which was an excess of expenditure) amounting, to £l4. The rate uncollected was £B, which conspired favourably with last year. Taking the rates outstanding and un- collected for the three accounts—the General Improve- ment, the Highways and Water—they had a total of £46 against £6OO. outstanding ten years ago. (Hear, hear.) He thought that spoke very favourably for the way Mr. Houghton had carried out his du-ies, and he was to be commended for the manner in which he got the money in. The loans outstanding on the 31st March last amounted to £22,382 18s 81. At the beginning of the last financial year, there was outstanding £20,273 6; 71, but to that must be added loans taken up for the gas ex- tentiont. amounting to £3,500. They had paid off £1 390 7e 111 during the ve .r so that the total cI amount outstanng was £22,383 in round figures. There was owing on the water department £733 6481 and on the gas department, £10,003 13s 4d, giving a total of £22,382 18 81. pe,s4. jug on to the estimates for the current year they found that tbheassessable value for the General Imorovement Rate d increased by £1767, the total assessable value being £50.292 7s 6i against £48,525 71 61 last year. It was proposed as would be noticsd from the agenda to levy a general improvement rate of is 11 in the pound. That amount was estimated to produce £2,721 3; 51, but other sources of income ageregated together are es- timated to produce £1,315 12s, making a total income of £4.039 154 51. The estimated ex- penditure was £3,785 7s 4d, which would leave a balance of £254 8; 14 towards the deficit on last year's estimate of £430. That was practically the sole reasen why the rates were to be advanced this year from is 11 to 23. It was so that they would not go on increasing debts without paying them and liquidating their liabili- ties year by year. When one year's estimate was excee led by £3OO or £4OO it was their duty in the follow- ing year to levy a rate that would meet the increased expenditure. If they did not exceed their estimates they would be able Co wipe off the deficit of last year and still have a margin of £B7. He had been looking through the Financial Year book and had taken'24 local authorities in Lanca- shire which in many respects could be said to be cm- parable to their own in population or rateable value,aad I he found that with two exceptions they had the lowest rates. It seems to me ifi we sit here all night we shall be no nearer. Each schoo would please itself. The: CHAIRMAN : That ic what we should all like to know, but the School Committee are anxious that there should be a uniform holiday. We don't mind whether it includes the Walking Day or Bank Holiday. The Rev. E. R. TARBUCH expressed a desire,if possible,

to support Canon Willis's proposition, mostly -because he was very strongly in sympathy with the teachers who were just starting their permanent teachers' life after leaving college, but practically that night they were bound at St. Paul's to follow the neighbouring schools. The Rev. F. A. SCRAETON said that in his parish the decision come to last sear would be adhered to. The CHAIRMAN : Christ Church is a somewhat iso- lated parish. What Lltchford does will not influence the decision of the meeting at all. What we want as a School Attendance Committee is one date. Mr. JAMES EVANS spoke in favour of the meeting com- ing to some unAnicnous decision on the matter. Mr. J. D. BIICKTaN slid the decision of the meeting that evening would have little effect that year, as their arrangements ha ,i been already made,but it might effect next year's holidays. It would be a pity to vote on the Rector's resolution that evening. The RECTOR said that under the circumstances, with the consent of his seconder, he would withdraw the resolution. This course was adopted. PUPIL TEACHERS' APPRENTICESHIP. _

MORLEY OTEVENSON : Yes, but we have always found the Inspectors willing to fall in with any suggestion to meet the conveniecce of the town. Mr. W. F. MONKS s..conded, Mr. JOLLEY supported,and the resolution was unanimously agreed to. The RECTOR : Who wdl bring the matter under the notice of the Ingpectors ? A suggestion was made that the task should he under- taken by the School Committee. (113ar, hear.) The CHAIRMAN : It would, perhaps, come with greater f4rce from the c)airnittae, and if it is your pleasure we will roport to the inspectors and ask their approval. (Hear, hear.)

THE STIPEND QUESTION.

The RECTOR then introduced the third question, namely, a uniform rate for stipend for pupil teachers. He proposed that boys should begin as candidates at £l2 103 ; anu go up by £2 103 0,1 a year ; girls £7 10.4 0.1, and go up to £lO ; second year, boys, £l7 103 Od, girls £l2 103 0,1 ; third year, boys £2O, girls £l5. ac said that between the completion of the apprenticeship, and entering into college they should be guided by circumstances whether there was to be an increase or not. All depended upon the fact whether the pupil teachers wish ed to stay, or the managers wished them to do so. The managers should pay, in addition, school fees for attendance at the central classes. How- ever, he thought the meeting seemed to consider this a purely business matter that should not be settled by then. _ _ _

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

WORKINGMEN'S MINSION SUNDAY SCHOOL.

Th 9 anniversary 3erv.ces in connection with the above Sunday School, were held on Sunday last, when the put pit was occupied, both morning and evening, by Mr. & El. Lowe (Student of Didsbury College). In the afro. _

noon a miisical service was rendered 'by the chillren and choir, and Mr. R. A. Naylor, delivered an 1/410q0ent and impressive address. Solos were rendered by Miss Armstrong, Mr. G. Gaugh and Mr.. Elassall. The con- gregations at each service was very good (especially aftern on and evening) and the collections were above the average. Great praise is due to the Choirmaster and Organist (Vlr. S. Hassall and Mr. F. Atkinson), for the efficient manner in which they had trained the children for the occasioß. the singing being excellent. ST. ALBIN'S CHURCH, BENSEY-STREET. SERMON ON " ar ruzimsu."

The annual sermons in aid of the schools were:preached in St. Alban's Church, Bewsey-street, on Sunday. There was solemn high mass in the morning. The celebrant was the Rev. Father F. A. Crew, with Father H. V. Folding (deacon) and Father J. P. Whittle (sub-deacon). The altar was very tastefully decorated. The music of the mass was Beetnov,n in C, and it was excellently rendered. The soloists were Miss Rigby and Miss A. Eckersley (soprano), Mrs. .1. B. Caldwell (alto), Mr. J. Bate (tenor), and Mr. P. Warburton (bass). Mr. J. B. Caldwell presi led at the organ. The preacher morning and ev-ning was the Very Rev. J. Proctor, Provincial of the D rninican Order in England. The subject of the morning sermon was " The perpetual life of the Catholic Church." In the ewnicg there were devotions of the rosary, followed by the sermon and solemn bene- diction of the Blessed Sacrament. Miss McClew sang " ave Maria" and Mr. J. Bate "Te Prego." "Preaching upon the subject" Kitualism in the Catholic Church "Father Proctor said he could well understand an i syrnpathiz, a bit with the wonderment of a stranger to their Catholic faith and Catholic practices when he went for the first time into one ot their Catholic Churches and saw their rites and ceremonies. He could well un- derstand him exclaiming what does it mean, if it has any meaning at all ? And yet he would say it must have a meaning,because he knew that the men whom he saw approving of what was going on were not fools. There must, he would r fleet, be something in the ritual of the Catholic Church. Wnat did it mean ? What was the secret ot the Catholic religion? Why did they build gorgeous churches and adorn them as beat they could ? Why had they flowers and candles and beautiful music, and why did they go through those external rites and ceremonies ? There was a raison for it all. Catholics knew what it meant.

They knew that ritual was a perpetual servant, and that it preached to them more eloquently then the words of eloquent men. It taught them their very faith. They had to give to God not only the affection of their will bat the wership of their external selves. All that went to the mind pissed through the senses of the b edy se the Church used ritual to instruct man through his bodily senses in order to gain his thought, and thro ith his thoueht his heart and will to God. In a few words that was the meaning! of the ritu of the Catholic C lurch. There were two dangers in the use of ritual. People either misunderstanding or not understanding cote- demned that which they did not understand- They were told in the Holy Scripture that when David danced with all his might before the ark the daughter of Saul sneered at him. She di .l not under- stand his external act of reverence to God, and to she sneered. There were people who did not understand the ritual of the Catholic Church, and like the daughter of Saul they sneered. They did not study, yet they con- demned. If they wanted to know what Catholicism meant they sh mid come and a 4k. There was a danger from the misuse of ritual. By that be meant people resting upon the external thing, loeking on the outward shell and not seeing the kernel within. That wai making ritual a religion, and he feaud there were many in England who did that. They di I not understand as Catholics understood the doctrine implied by ritual. They did not unders and that it was only an outward sign of seinething that was intended to be con- veyed to the mind. The ritualism of the Catholic Church was external wo• ship wadded to interior worship. In the Bible ritual was prescribed and practiced every- where. The preacher gave numerous inetaeces, queting particularly incidents which occurred during the lite of our Lord. l Lordr it . wHaeswweliettal:inn ttmiy mustohesayttiehat they could t ledniot bide instanced the outward expression of a child's love for its father and mother. Speaking of the love of God, he said that if there was a light within it would be shown extern- ally. Ritual, therefore, was the external manifestation ot interior love. They liked to give to G d nature's best gifts. Lot, hem give then to God riot oily Lho goo 3 bmt the best. Lot them take their best possessions and use them in his service. Ritual was also an external bond of unity amongs all Catholics. A Protestant going into a foreign country would not understand the service of his church if he did not know the language. There was a common tongue and the same service exactly in all the Catholic Churches throughout the world, and an English Catholic visiting a foreign country or a foreigner in England would be able to follow the service as easily as though he were at home. Ritualism was one ot the wisest institutions which God had given to the Church, and its language was understood by God if misunder- stood by men.

GOSPEL HALL.

On Sunday Messrs. W. Roden and Frank Taylor con- ducted the service here. Mr. Roden speaking from Luke 2. 49, " Wit ye not that I must be ablut my Father's business?" pointed out that profit and lose were the important considerations of a business man. The business of the Lord Jesus Christ was to work out His Father's will in the salvation of man. He put every- thing he bad into the business and it has not failed yet. He receives the profits when sinners are saved. He asks the business man," What shall it profit it you gain the world and lose your soul ? " God values the soul of man more previous than gold, but some men sell their souls for a few piecesef gold. Will it pay to barter the soul for a few short years of so called pleasure? Mr.F. Taylor from Mark 10 49 pointed out how Christ gives sight to the spiritually blind. He longs to rnest the need of man but man will try everything before be comes to Christ to supply his need. If man gets not spiritual sight in this life be will be shut into the dark- ness for ever.

An alarming accident occurred at Wtl,tipool yesterday morning in Powis Castle Park, the Earl of Powiti'S picturesque Mantgomery cl,mense, resulting in onedeath and serious injuries to three children. The Montgomery- shire Militia were being drilled in the park, and their exercises were, as usual, watched by a crowd of people, including a number of children. Suddenly an old oak tree fell, and four children were tuned beneath RS branches. One girl aged ..bou, fourteen, was killed oa the spot,ber head being smashed,and three other girls VI ceived such serious injuries that their lives are despaired of.
